{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01J1ivUJ13C5E2LuE9jddvWh","type":"tool_result","content":"{\"content\":\"300:  *                 }\\n301:  *                 long lastOffset = partitionRecords.get(partitionRecords.size() - 1).offset();\\n302:  *                 consumer.commitSync(Collections.singletonMap(partition, new OffsetAndMetadata(lastOffset + 1)));\\n303:  *             }\\n304:  *         }\\n305:  *     } finally {\\n306:  *       consumer.close();\\n307:  *     }\\n308:  * </pre>\\n309:  *\\n310:  * <b>Note: The committed offset should always be the offset of the next message that your application will read.</b>\\n311:  * Thus, when calling {@link #commitSync(Map) commitSync(offsets)} you should add one to the offset of the last message processed.\\n312:  *\\n313:  * <h4><a name=\\\"manualassignment\\\">Manual Partition Assignment</a></h4>\\n314:  *\\n315:  * In the previous examples, we subscribed to the topics we were interested in and let Kafka dynamically assign a\\n316:  * fair share of the partitions for those topics based on the active consumers in the group. However, in\\n317:  * some cases you may need finer control over the specific partitions that are assigned. For example:\\n318:  * <p>\\n319:  * <ul>\\n320:  * <li>If the process is maintaining some kind of local state associated with that partition (like a\\n321:  * local on-disk key-value store), then it should only get records for the partition it is maintaining on disk.\\n322:  * <li>If the process itself is highly available and will be restarted if it fails (perhaps using a\\n323:  * cluster management framework like YARN, Mesos, or AWS facilities, or as part of a stream processing framework). In\\n324:  * this case there is no need for Kafka to detect the failure and reassign the partition since the consuming process\\n325:  * will be restarted on another machine.\\n326:  * </ul>\\n327:  * <p>\\n328:  * To use this mode, instead of subscribing to the topic using {@link #subscribe(Collection) subscribe}, you just call\\n329:  * {@link #assign(Collection)} with the full list of partitions that you want to consume.\\n330:  *\\n331:  * <pre>\\n332:  *     String topic = &quot;foo&quot;;\\n333:  *     TopicPartition partition0 = new TopicPartition(topic, 0);\\n334:  *     TopicPartition partition1 = new TopicPartition(topic, 1);\\n335:  *     consumer.assign(Arrays.asList(partition0, partition1));\\n336:  * </pre>\\n337:  *\\n338:  * Once assigned, you can call {@link #poll(Duration) poll} in a loop, just as in the preceding examples to consume\\n339:  * records. The group that the consumer specifies is still used for committing offsets, but now the set of partitions\\n340:  * will only change with another call to {@link #assign(Collection) assign}. Manual partition assignment does\\n341:  * not use group coordination, so consumer failures will not cause assigned partitions to be rebalanced. Each consumer\\n342:  * acts independently even if it shares a groupId with another consumer. To avoid offset commit conflicts, you should\\n343:  * usually ensure that the groupId is unique for each consumer instance.\\n344:  * <p>\\n345:  * Note that it isn't possible to mix manual partition assignment (i.e. using {@link #assign(Collection) assign})\\n346:  * with dynamic partition assignment through topic subscription (i.e. using {@link #subscribe(Collection) subscribe}).\\n347:  *\\n348:  * <h4><a name=\\\"rebalancecallback\\\">Storing Offsets Outside Kafka</h4>\\n349:  *\\n350:  * The consumer application need not use Kafka's built-in offset storage, it can store offsets in a store of its own\\n351:  * choosing. The primary use case for this is allowing the application to store both the offset and the results of the\\n352:  * consumption in the same system in a way that both the results and offsets are stored atomically. This is not always\\n353:  * possible, but when it is it will make the consumption fully atomic and give \\\"exactly once\\\" semantics that are\\n354:  * stronger than the default \\\"at-least once\\\" semantics you get with Kafka's offset commit functionality.\\n355:  * <p>\\n356:  * Here are a couple of examples of this type of usage:\\n357:  * <ul>\\n358:  * <li>If the results of the consumption are being stored in a relational database, storing the offset in the database\\n359:  * as well can allow committing both the results and offset in a single transaction. Thus either the transaction will\\n360:  * succeed and the offset will be updated based on what was consumed or the result will not be stored and the offset\\n361:  * won't be updated.\\n362:  * <li>If the results are being stored in a local store it may be possible to store the offset there as well. For\\n363:  * example a search index could be built by subscribing to a particular partition and storing both the offset and the\\n364:  * indexed data together. If this is done in a way that is atomic, it is often possible to have it be the case that even\\n365:  * if a crash occurs that causes unsync'd data to be lost, whatever is left has the corresponding offset stored as well.\\n366:  * This means that in this case the indexing process that comes back having lost recent updates just resumes indexing\\n367:  * from what it has ensuring that no updates are lost.\\n368:  * </ul>\\n369:  * <p>\\n370:  * Each record comes with its own offset, so to manage your own offset you just need to do the following:\\n371:  *\\n372:  * <ul>\\n373:  * <li>Configure <code>enable.auto.commit=false</code>\\n374:  * <li>Use the offset provided with each {@link ConsumerRecord} to save your position.\\n375:  * <li>On restart restore the position of the consumer using {@link #seek(TopicPartition, long)}.\\n376:  * </ul>\\n377:  *\\n378:  * <p>\\n379:  * This type of usage is simplest when the partition assignment is also done manually (this would be likely in the\\n380:  * search index use case described above). If the partition assignment is done automatically special care is\\n381:  * needed to handle the case where partition assignments change. This can be done by providing a\\n382:  * {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener} instance in the call to {@link #subscribe(Collection, ConsumerRebalanceListener)}\\n383:  * and {@link #subscribe(Pattern, ConsumerRebalanceListener)}.\\n384:  * For example, when partitions are taken from a consumer the consumer will want to commit its offset for those partitions by\\n385:  * implementing {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener#onPartitionsRevoked(Collection)}. When partitions are assigned to a\\n386:  * consumer, the consumer will want to look up the offset for those new partitions and correctly initialize the consumer\\n387:  * to that position by implementing {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener#onPartitionsAssigned(Collection)}.\\n388:  * <p>\\n389:  * Another common use for {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener} is to flush any caches the application maintains for\\n390:  * partitions that are moved elsewhere.\\n391:  *\\n392:  * <h4>Controlling The Consumer's Position</h4>\\n393:  *\\n394:  * In most use cases the consumer will simply consume records from beginning to end, periodically committing its\\n395:  * position (either automatically or manually). However Kafka allows the consumer to manually control its position,\\n396:  * moving forward or backwards in a partition at will. This means a consumer can re-consume older records, or skip to\\n397:  * the most recent records without actually consuming the intermediate records.\\n398:  * <p>\\n399:  * There are several instances where manually controlling the consumer's position can be useful.\\n400:  * <p>\\n401:  * One case is for time-sensitive record processing it may make sense for a consumer that falls far enough behind to not\\n402:  * attempt to catch up processing all records, but rather just skip to the most recent records.\\n403:  * <p>\\n404:  * Another use case is for a system that maintains local state as described in the previous section. In such a system\\n405:  * the consumer will want to initialize its position on start-up to whatever is contained in the local store. Likewise\\n406:  * if the local state is destroyed (say because the disk is lost) the state may be recreated on a new machine by\\n407:  * re-consuming all the data and recreating the state (assuming that Kafka is retaining sufficient history).\\n408:  * <p>\\n409:  * Kafka allows specifying the position using {@link #seek(TopicPartition, long)} to specify the new position. Special\\n410:  * methods for seeking to the earliest and latest offset the server maintains are also available (\\n411:  * {@link #seekToBeginning(Collection)} and {@link #seekToEnd(Collection)} respectively).\\n412:  *\\n413:  * <h4>Consumption Flow Control</h4>\\n414:  *\\n415:  * If a consumer is assigned multiple partitions to fetch data from, it will try to consume from all of them at the same time,\\n416:  * effectively giving these partitions the same priority for consumption. However in some cases consumers may want to\\n417:  * first focus on fetching from some subset of the assigned partitions at full speed, and only start fetching other partitions\\n418:  * when these partitions have few or no data to consume.\\n419:  *\\n420:  * <p>\\n421:  * One of such cases is stream processing, where processor fetches from two topics and performs the join on these two streams.\\n422:  * When one of the topics is long lagging behind the other, the processor would like to pause fetching from the ahead topic\\n423:  * in order to get the lagging stream to catch up. Another example is bootstraping upon consumer starting up where there are\\n424:  * a lot of history data to catch up, the applications usually want to get the latest data on some of the topics before consider\\n425:  * fetching other topics.\\n426:  *\\n427:  * <p>\\n428:  * Kafka supports dynamic controlling of consumption flows by using {@link #pause(Collection)} and {@link #resume(Collection)}\\n429:  * to pause the consumption on the specified assigned partitions and resume the consumption\\n430:  * on the specified paused partitions respectively in the future {@link #poll(Duration)} calls.\\n431:  *\\n432:  * <h3>Reading Transactional Messages</h3>\\n433:  *\\n434:  * <p>\\n435:  * Transactions were introduced in Kafka 0.11.0 wherein applications can write to multiple topics and partitions atomically.\\n436:  * In order for this to work, consumers reading from these partitions should be configured to only read committed data.\\n437:  * This can be achieved by setting the {@code isolation.level=read_committed} in the consumer's configuration.\\n438:  *\\n439:  * <p>\\n440:  * In <code>read_committed</code> mode, the consumer will read only those transactional messages which have been\\n441:  * successfully committed. It will continue to read non-transactional messages as before. There is no client-side\\n442:  * buffering in <code>read_committed</code> mode. Instead, the end offset of a partition for a <code>read_committed</code>\\n443:  * consumer would be the offset of the first message in the partition belonging to an open transaction. This offset\\n444:  * is known as the 'Last Stable Offset'(LSO).</p>\\n445:  *\\n446:  * <p>\\n447:  * A {@code read_committed} consumer will only read up to the LSO and filter out any transactional\\n448:  * messages which have been aborted. The LSO also affects the behavior of {@link #seekToEnd(Collection)} and\\n449:  * {@link #endOffsets(Collection)} for {@code read_committed} consumers, details of which are in each method's documentation.\\n450:  * Finally, the fetch lag metrics are also adjusted to be relative to the LSO for {@code read_committed} consumers.\\n451:  *\\n452:  * <p>\\n453:  * Partitions with transactional messages will include commit or abort markers which indicate the result of a transaction.\\n454:  * There markers are not returned to applications, yet have an offset in the log. As a result, applications reading from\\n455:  * topics with transactional messages will see gaps in the consumed offsets. These missing messages would be the transaction\\n456:  * markers, and they are filtered out for consumers in both isolation levels. Additionally, applications using\\n457:  * {@code read_committed} consumers may also see gaps due to aborted transactions, since those messages would not\\n458:  * be returned by the consumer and yet would have valid offsets.\\n459:  *\\n460:  * <h3><a name=\\\"multithreaded\\\">Multi-threaded Processing</a></h3>\\n461:  *\\n462:  * The Kafka consumer is NOT thread-safe. All network I/O happens in the thread of the application\\n463:  * making the call. It is the responsibility of the user to ensure that multi-threaded access\\n464:  * is properly synchronized. Un-synchronized access will result in {@link ConcurrentModificationException}.\\n465:  *\\n466:  * <p>\\n467:  * The only exception to this rule is {@link #wakeup()}, which can safely be used from an external thread to\\n468:  * interrupt an active operation. In this case, a {@link org.apache.kafka.common.errors.WakeupException} will be\\n469:  * thrown from the thread blocking on the operation. This can be used to shutdown the consumer from another thread.\\n470:  * The following snippet shows the typical pattern:\\n471:  *\\n472:  * <pre>\\n473:  * public class KafkaConsumerRunner implements Runnable {\\n474:  *     private final AtomicBoolean closed = new AtomicBoolean(false);\\n475:  *     private final KafkaConsumer consumer;\\n476:  *\\n477:  *     public KafkaConsumerRunner(KafkaConsumer consumer) {\\n478:  *       this.consumer = consumer;\\n479:  *     }\\n480:  *\\n481:  *     {@literal}@Override\\n482:  *     public void run() {\\n483:  *         try {\\n484:  *             consumer.subscribe(Arrays.asList(\\\"topic\\\"));\\n485:  *             while (!closed.get()) {\\n486:  *                 ConsumerRecords records = consumer.poll(Duration.ofMillis(10000));\\n487:  *                 // Handle new records\\n488:  *             }\\n489:  *         } catch (WakeupException e) {\\n490:  *             // Ignore exception if closing\\n491:  *             if (!closed.get()) throw e;\\n492:  *         } finally {\\n493:  *             consumer.close();\\n494:  *         }\\n495:  *     }\\n496:  *\\n497:  *     // Shutdown hook which can be called from a separate thread\\n498:  *     public void shutdown() {\\n499:  *         closed.set(true);\\n500:  *         consumer.wakeup();\\n501:  *     }\\n502:  * }\\n503:  * </pre>\\n504:  *\\n505:  * Then in a separate thread, the consumer can be shutdown by setting the closed flag and waking up the consumer.\\n506:  *\\n507:  * <p>\\n508:  * <pre>\\n509:  *     closed.set(true);\\n510:  *     consumer.wakeup();\\n511:  * </pre>\\n512:  *\\n513:  * <p>\\n514:  * Note that while it is possible to use thread interrupts instead of {@link #wakeup()} to abort a blocking operation\\n515:  * (in which case, {@link InterruptException} will be raised), we discourage their use since they may cause a clean\\n516:  * shutdown of the consumer to be aborted. Interrupts are mainly supported for those cases where using {@link #wakeup()}\\n517:  * is impossible, e.g. when a consumer thread is managed by code that is unaware of the Kafka client.\\n518:  *\\n519:  * <p>\\n520:  * We have intentionally avoided implementing a particular threading model for processing. This leaves several\\n521:  * options for implementing multi-threaded processing of records.\\n522:  *\\n523:  * <h4>1. One Consumer Per Thread</h4>\\n524:  *\\n525:  * A simple option is to give each thread its own consumer instance. Here are the pros and cons of this approach:\\n526:  * <ul>\\n527:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It is the easiest to implement\\n528:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It is often the fastest as no inter-thread co-ordination is needed\\n529:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It makes in-order processing on a per-partition basis very easy to implement (each thread just\\n530:  * processes messages in the order it receives them).\\n531:  * <li><b>CON</b>: More consumers means more TCP connections to the cluster (one per thread). In general Kafka handles\\n532:  * connections very efficiently so this is generally a small cost.\\n533:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Multiple consumers means more requests being sent to the server and slightly less batching of data\\n534:  * which can cause some drop in I/O throughput.\\n535:  * <li><b>CON</b>: The number of total threads across all processes will be limited by the total number of partitions.\\n536:  * </ul>\\n537:  *\\n538:  * <h4>2. Decouple Consumption and Processing</h4>\\n539:  *\\n540:  * Another alternative is to have one or more consumer threads that do all data consumption and hands off\\n541:  * {@link ConsumerRecords} instances to a blocking queue consumed by a pool of processor threads that actually handle\\n542:  * the record processing.\\n543:  *\\n544:  * This option likewise has pros and cons:\\n545:  * <ul>\\n546:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: This option allows independently scaling the number of consumers and processors. This makes it\\n547:  * possible to have a single consumer that feeds many processor threads, avoiding any limitation on partitions.\\n548:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Guaranteeing order across the processors requires particular care as the threads will execute\\n549:  * independently an earlier chunk of data may actually be processed after a later chunk of data just due to the luck of\\n550:  * thread execution timing. For processing that has no ordering requirements this is not a problem.\\n551:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Manually committing the position becomes harder as it requires that all threads co-ordinate to ensure\\n552:  * that processing is complete for that partition.\\n553:  * </ul>\\n554:  *\\n555:  * There are many possible variations on this approach. For example each processor thread can have its own queue, and\\n556:  * the consumer threads can hash into these queues using the TopicPartition to ensure in-order consumption and simplify\\n557:  * commit.\\n558:  */\\n559: public class KafkaConsumer<K, V> implements Consumer<K, V> {\\n560: \\n561:     private static final String CLIENT_ID_METRIC_TAG = \\\"client-id\\\";\\n562:     private static final long NO_CURRENT_THREAD = -1L;\\n563:     private static final String JMX_PREFIX = \\\"kafka.consumer\\\";\\n564:     static final long DEFAULT_CLOSE_TIMEOUT_MS = 30 * 1000;\\n565: \\n566:     // Visible for testing\\n567:     final Metrics metrics;\\n568:     final KafkaConsumerMetrics kafkaConsumerMetrics;\\n569: \\n570:     private Logger log;\\n571:     private final String clientId;\\n572:     private final Optional<String> groupId;\\n573:     private final ConsumerCoordinator coordinator;\\n574:     private final Deserializer<K> keyDeserializer;\\n575:     private final Deserializer<V> valueDeserializer;\\n576:     private final Fetcher<K, V> fetcher;\\n577:     private final ConsumerInterceptors<K, V> interceptors;\\n578:     private final IsolationLevel isolationLevel;\\n579: \\n580:     private final Time time;\\n581:     private final ConsumerNetworkClient client;\\n582:     private final SubscriptionState subscriptions;\\n583:     private final ConsumerMetadata metadata;\\n584:     private final long retryBackoffMs;\\n585:     private final long requestTimeoutMs;\\n586:     private final int defaultApiTimeoutMs;\\n587:     private volatile boolean closed = false;\\n588:     private List<ConsumerPartitionAssignor> assignors;\\n589: \\n590:     // currentThread holds the threadId of the current thread accessing KafkaConsumer\\n591:     // and is used to prevent multi-threaded access\\n592:     private final AtomicLong currentThread = new AtomicLong(NO_CURRENT_THREAD);\\n593:     // refcount is used to allow reentrant access by the thread who has acquired currentThread\\n594:     private final AtomicInteger refcount = new AtomicInteger(0);\\n595: \\n596:     // to keep from repeatedly scanning subscriptions in poll(), cache the Valid configuration strings\\n601: \",\"url\":\"https://sourcegraph.sourcegraph.com/github.com/sourcegraph-testing/kafka@a61b998c5df7ca9baccc5d966cb1e95108cbdee9/-/blob/clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/clients/consumer/KafkaConsumer.java%3FL300-600\"}"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c81f12f9-964b-4bd1-aec9-0f801cd8aa7d","uuid":"c5619b44-3ed7-4bbd-956d-2a942083c189","tool_use_result":{"content":"{\"content\":\"300:  *                 }\\n301:  *                 long lastOffset = partitionRecords.get(partitionRecords.size() - 1).offset();\\n302:  *                 consumer.commitSync(Collections.singletonMap(partition, new OffsetAndMetadata(lastOffset + 1)));\\n303:  *             }\\n304:  *         }\\n305:  *     } finally {\\n306:  *       consumer.close();\\n307:  *     }\\n308:  * </pre>\\n309:  *\\n310:  * <b>Note: The committed offset should always be the offset of the next message that your application will read.</b>\\n311:  * Thus, when calling {@link #commitSync(Map) commitSync(offsets)} you should add one to the offset of the last message processed.\\n312:  *\\n313:  * <h4><a name=\\\"manualassignment\\\">Manual Partition Assignment</a></h4>\\n314:  *\\n315:  * In the previous examples, we subscribed to the topics we were interested in and let Kafka dynamically assign a\\n316:  * fair share of the partitions for those topics based on the active consumers in the group. However, in\\n317:  * some cases you may need finer control over the specific partitions that are assigned. For example:\\n318:  * <p>\\n319:  * <ul>\\n320:  * <li>If the process is maintaining some kind of local state associated with that partition (like a\\n321:  * local on-disk key-value store), then it should only get records for the partition it is maintaining on disk.\\n322:  * <li>If the process itself is highly available and will be restarted if it fails (perhaps using a\\n323:  * cluster management framework like YARN, Mesos, or AWS facilities, or as part of a stream processing framework). In\\n324:  * this case there is no need for Kafka to detect the failure and reassign the partition since the consuming process\\n325:  * will be restarted on another machine.\\n326:  * </ul>\\n327:  * <p>\\n328:  * To use this mode, instead of subscribing to the topic using {@link #subscribe(Collection) subscribe}, you just call\\n329:  * {@link #assign(Collection)} with the full list of partitions that you want to consume.\\n330:  *\\n331:  * <pre>\\n332:  *     String topic = &quot;foo&quot;;\\n333:  *     TopicPartition partition0 = new TopicPartition(topic, 0);\\n334:  *     TopicPartition partition1 = new TopicPartition(topic, 1);\\n335:  *     consumer.assign(Arrays.asList(partition0, partition1));\\n336:  * </pre>\\n337:  *\\n338:  * Once assigned, you can call {@link #poll(Duration) poll} in a loop, just as in the preceding examples to consume\\n339:  * records. The group that the consumer specifies is still used for committing offsets, but now the set of partitions\\n340:  * will only change with another call to {@link #assign(Collection) assign}. Manual partition assignment does\\n341:  * not use group coordination, so consumer failures will not cause assigned partitions to be rebalanced. Each consumer\\n342:  * acts independently even if it shares a groupId with another consumer. To avoid offset commit conflicts, you should\\n343:  * usually ensure that the groupId is unique for each consumer instance.\\n344:  * <p>\\n345:  * Note that it isn't possible to mix manual partition assignment (i.e. using {@link #assign(Collection) assign})\\n346:  * with dynamic partition assignment through topic subscription (i.e. using {@link #subscribe(Collection) subscribe}).\\n347:  *\\n348:  * <h4><a name=\\\"rebalancecallback\\\">Storing Offsets Outside Kafka</h4>\\n349:  *\\n350:  * The consumer application need not use Kafka's built-in offset storage, it can store offsets in a store of its own\\n351:  * choosing. The primary use case for this is allowing the application to store both the offset and the results of the\\n352:  * consumption in the same system in a way that both the results and offsets are stored atomically. This is not always\\n353:  * possible, but when it is it will make the consumption fully atomic and give \\\"exactly once\\\" semantics that are\\n354:  * stronger than the default \\\"at-least once\\\" semantics you get with Kafka's offset commit functionality.\\n355:  * <p>\\n356:  * Here are a couple of examples of this type of usage:\\n357:  * <ul>\\n358:  * <li>If the results of the consumption are being stored in a relational database, storing the offset in the database\\n359:  * as well can allow committing both the results and offset in a single transaction. Thus either the transaction will\\n360:  * succeed and the offset will be updated based on what was consumed or the result will not be stored and the offset\\n361:  * won't be updated.\\n362:  * <li>If the results are being stored in a local store it may be possible to store the offset there as well. For\\n363:  * example a search index could be built by subscribing to a particular partition and storing both the offset and the\\n364:  * indexed data together. If this is done in a way that is atomic, it is often possible to have it be the case that even\\n365:  * if a crash occurs that causes unsync'd data to be lost, whatever is left has the corresponding offset stored as well.\\n366:  * This means that in this case the indexing process that comes back having lost recent updates just resumes indexing\\n367:  * from what it has ensuring that no updates are lost.\\n368:  * </ul>\\n369:  * <p>\\n370:  * Each record comes with its own offset, so to manage your own offset you just need to do the following:\\n371:  *\\n372:  * <ul>\\n373:  * <li>Configure <code>enable.auto.commit=false</code>\\n374:  * <li>Use the offset provided with each {@link ConsumerRecord} to save your position.\\n375:  * <li>On restart restore the position of the consumer using {@link #seek(TopicPartition, long)}.\\n376:  * </ul>\\n377:  *\\n378:  * <p>\\n379:  * This type of usage is simplest when the partition assignment is also done manually (this would be likely in the\\n380:  * search index use case described above). If the partition assignment is done automatically special care is\\n381:  * needed to handle the case where partition assignments change. This can be done by providing a\\n382:  * {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener} instance in the call to {@link #subscribe(Collection, ConsumerRebalanceListener)}\\n383:  * and {@link #subscribe(Pattern, ConsumerRebalanceListener)}.\\n384:  * For example, when partitions are taken from a consumer the consumer will want to commit its offset for those partitions by\\n385:  * implementing {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener#onPartitionsRevoked(Collection)}. When partitions are assigned to a\\n386:  * consumer, the consumer will want to look up the offset for those new partitions and correctly initialize the consumer\\n387:  * to that position by implementing {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener#onPartitionsAssigned(Collection)}.\\n388:  * <p>\\n389:  * Another common use for {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener} is to flush any caches the application maintains for\\n390:  * partitions that are moved elsewhere.\\n391:  *\\n392:  * <h4>Controlling The Consumer's Position</h4>\\n393:  *\\n394:  * In most use cases the consumer will simply consume records from beginning to end, periodically committing its\\n395:  * position (either automatically or manually). However Kafka allows the consumer to manually control its position,\\n396:  * moving forward or backwards in a partition at will. This means a consumer can re-consume older records, or skip to\\n397:  * the most recent records without actually consuming the intermediate records.\\n398:  * <p>\\n399:  * There are several instances where manually controlling the consumer's position can be useful.\\n400:  * <p>\\n401:  * One case is for time-sensitive record processing it may make sense for a consumer that falls far enough behind to not\\n402:  * attempt to catch up processing all records, but rather just skip to the most recent records.\\n403:  * <p>\\n404:  * Another use case is for a system that maintains local state as described in the previous section. In such a system\\n405:  * the consumer will want to initialize its position on start-up to whatever is contained in the local store. Likewise\\n406:  * if the local state is destroyed (say because the disk is lost) the state may be recreated on a new machine by\\n407:  * re-consuming all the data and recreating the state (assuming that Kafka is retaining sufficient history).\\n408:  * <p>\\n409:  * Kafka allows specifying the position using {@link #seek(TopicPartition, long)} to specify the new position. Special\\n410:  * methods for seeking to the earliest and latest offset the server maintains are also available (\\n411:  * {@link #seekToBeginning(Collection)} and {@link #seekToEnd(Collection)} respectively).\\n412:  *\\n413:  * <h4>Consumption Flow Control</h4>\\n414:  *\\n415:  * If a consumer is assigned multiple partitions to fetch data from, it will try to consume from all of them at the same time,\\n416:  * effectively giving these partitions the same priority for consumption. However in some cases consumers may want to\\n417:  * first focus on fetching from some subset of the assigned partitions at full speed, and only start fetching other partitions\\n418:  * when these partitions have few or no data to consume.\\n419:  *\\n420:  * <p>\\n421:  * One of such cases is stream processing, where processor fetches from two topics and performs the join on these two streams.\\n422:  * When one of the topics is long lagging behind the other, the processor would like to pause fetching from the ahead topic\\n423:  * in order to get the lagging stream to catch up. Another example is bootstraping upon consumer starting up where there are\\n424:  * a lot of history data to catch up, the applications usually want to get the latest data on some of the topics before consider\\n425:  * fetching other topics.\\n426:  *\\n427:  * <p>\\n428:  * Kafka supports dynamic controlling of consumption flows by using {@link #pause(Collection)} and {@link #resume(Collection)}\\n429:  * to pause the consumption on the specified assigned partitions and resume the consumption\\n430:  * on the specified paused partitions respectively in the future {@link #poll(Duration)} calls.\\n431:  *\\n432:  * <h3>Reading Transactional Messages</h3>\\n433:  *\\n434:  * <p>\\n435:  * Transactions were introduced in Kafka 0.11.0 wherein applications can write to multiple topics and partitions atomically.\\n436:  * In order for this to work, consumers reading from these partitions should be configured to only read committed data.\\n437:  * This can be achieved by setting the {@code isolation.level=read_committed} in the consumer's configuration.\\n438:  *\\n439:  * <p>\\n440:  * In <code>read_committed</code> mode, the consumer will read only those transactional messages which have been\\n441:  * successfully committed. It will continue to read non-transactional messages as before. There is no client-side\\n442:  * buffering in <code>read_committed</code> mode. Instead, the end offset of a partition for a <code>read_committed</code>\\n443:  * consumer would be the offset of the first message in the partition belonging to an open transaction. This offset\\n444:  * is known as the 'Last Stable Offset'(LSO).</p>\\n445:  *\\n446:  * <p>\\n447:  * A {@code read_committed} consumer will only read up to the LSO and filter out any transactional\\n448:  * messages which have been aborted. The LSO also affects the behavior of {@link #seekToEnd(Collection)} and\\n449:  * {@link #endOffsets(Collection)} for {@code read_committed} consumers, details of which are in each method's documentation.\\n450:  * Finally, the fetch lag metrics are also adjusted to be relative to the LSO for {@code read_committed} consumers.\\n451:  *\\n452:  * <p>\\n453:  * Partitions with transactional messages will include commit or abort markers which indicate the result of a transaction.\\n454:  * There markers are not returned to applications, yet have an offset in the log. As a result, applications reading from\\n455:  * topics with transactional messages will see gaps in the consumed offsets. These missing messages would be the transaction\\n456:  * markers, and they are filtered out for consumers in both isolation levels. Additionally, applications using\\n457:  * {@code read_committed} consumers may also see gaps due to aborted transactions, since those messages would not\\n458:  * be returned by the consumer and yet would have valid offsets.\\n459:  *\\n460:  * <h3><a name=\\\"multithreaded\\\">Multi-threaded Processing</a></h3>\\n461:  *\\n462:  * The Kafka consumer is NOT thread-safe. All network I/O happens in the thread of the application\\n463:  * making the call. It is the responsibility of the user to ensure that multi-threaded access\\n464:  * is properly synchronized. Un-synchronized access will result in {@link ConcurrentModificationException}.\\n465:  *\\n466:  * <p>\\n467:  * The only exception to this rule is {@link #wakeup()}, which can safely be used from an external thread to\\n468:  * interrupt an active operation. In this case, a {@link org.apache.kafka.common.errors.WakeupException} will be\\n469:  * thrown from the thread blocking on the operation. This can be used to shutdown the consumer from another thread.\\n470:  * The following snippet shows the typical pattern:\\n471:  *\\n472:  * <pre>\\n473:  * public class KafkaConsumerRunner implements Runnable {\\n474:  *     private final AtomicBoolean closed = new AtomicBoolean(false);\\n475:  *     private final KafkaConsumer consumer;\\n476:  *\\n477:  *     public KafkaConsumerRunner(KafkaConsumer consumer) {\\n478:  *       this.consumer = consumer;\\n479:  *     }\\n480:  *\\n481:  *     {@literal}@Override\\n482:  *     public void run() {\\n483:  *         try {\\n484:  *             consumer.subscribe(Arrays.asList(\\\"topic\\\"));\\n485:  *             while (!closed.get()) {\\n486:  *                 ConsumerRecords records = consumer.poll(Duration.ofMillis(10000));\\n487:  *                 // Handle new records\\n488:  *             }\\n489:  *         } catch (WakeupException e) {\\n490:  *             // Ignore exception if closing\\n491:  *             if (!closed.get()) throw e;\\n492:  *         } finally {\\n493:  *             consumer.close();\\n494:  *         }\\n495:  *     }\\n496:  *\\n497:  *     // Shutdown hook which can be called from a separate thread\\n498:  *     public void shutdown() {\\n499:  *         closed.set(true);\\n500:  *         consumer.wakeup();\\n501:  *     }\\n502:  * }\\n503:  * </pre>\\n504:  *\\n505:  * Then in a separate thread, the consumer can be shutdown by setting the closed flag and waking up the consumer.\\n506:  *\\n507:  * <p>\\n508:  * <pre>\\n509:  *     closed.set(true);\\n510:  *     consumer.wakeup();\\n511:  * </pre>\\n512:  *\\n513:  * <p>\\n514:  * Note that while it is possible to use thread interrupts instead of {@link #wakeup()} to abort a blocking operation\\n515:  * (in which case, {@link InterruptException} will be raised), we discourage their use since they may cause a clean\\n516:  * shutdown of the consumer to be aborted. Interrupts are mainly supported for those cases where using {@link #wakeup()}\\n517:  * is impossible, e.g. when a consumer thread is managed by code that is unaware of the Kafka client.\\n518:  *\\n519:  * <p>\\n520:  * We have intentionally avoided implementing a particular threading model for processing. This leaves several\\n521:  * options for implementing multi-threaded processing of records.\\n522:  *\\n523:  * <h4>1. One Consumer Per Thread</h4>\\n524:  *\\n525:  * A simple option is to give each thread its own consumer instance. Here are the pros and cons of this approach:\\n526:  * <ul>\\n527:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It is the easiest to implement\\n528:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It is often the fastest as no inter-thread co-ordination is needed\\n529:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It makes in-order processing on a per-partition basis very easy to implement (each thread just\\n530:  * processes messages in the order it receives them).\\n531:  * <li><b>CON</b>: More consumers means more TCP connections to the cluster (one per thread). In general Kafka handles\\n532:  * connections very efficiently so this is generally a small cost.\\n533:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Multiple consumers means more requests being sent to the server and slightly less batching of data\\n534:  * which can cause some drop in I/O throughput.\\n535:  * <li><b>CON</b>: The number of total threads across all processes will be limited by the total number of partitions.\\n536:  * </ul>\\n537:  *\\n538:  * <h4>2. Decouple Consumption and Processing</h4>\\n539:  *\\n540:  * Another alternative is to have one or more consumer threads that do all data consumption and hands off\\n541:  * {@link ConsumerRecords} instances to a blocking queue consumed by a pool of processor threads that actually handle\\n542:  * the record processing.\\n543:  *\\n544:  * This option likewise has pros and cons:\\n545:  * <ul>\\n546:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: This option allows independently scaling the number of consumers and processors. This makes it\\n547:  * possible to have a single consumer that feeds many processor threads, avoiding any limitation on partitions.\\n548:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Guaranteeing order across the processors requires particular care as the threads will execute\\n549:  * independently an earlier chunk of data may actually be processed after a later chunk of data just due to the luck of\\n550:  * thread execution timing. For processing that has no ordering requirements this is not a problem.\\n551:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Manually committing the position becomes harder as it requires that all threads co-ordinate to ensure\\n552:  * that processing is complete for that partition.\\n553:  * </ul>\\n554:  *\\n555:  * There are many possible variations on this approach. For example each processor thread can have its own queue, and\\n556:  * the consumer threads can hash into these queues using the TopicPartition to ensure in-order consumption and simplify\\n557:  * commit.\\n558:  */\\n559: public class KafkaConsumer<K, V> implements Consumer<K, V> {\\n560: \\n561:     private static final String CLIENT_ID_METRIC_TAG = \\\"client-id\\\";\\n562:     private result during metadata updates\\n597:     private boolean cachedSubscriptionHashAllFetchPositions;\\n598: \\n599:     /**\\n600:      * A consumer is instantiated by providing a set of key-value pairs as configuration. Valid configuration strings\\n601: \",\"url\":\"https://sourcegraph.sourcegraph.com/github.com/sourcegraph-testing/kafka@a61b998c5df7ca9baccc5d966cb1e95108cbdee9/-/blob/clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/clients/consumer/KafkaConsumer.java%3FL300-600\"}","structuredContent":{"content":"300:  *                 }\n301:  *                 long lastOffset = partitionRecords.get(partitionRecords.size() - 1).offset();\n302:  *                 consumer.commitSync(Collections.singletonMap(partition, new OffsetAndMetadata(lastOffset + 1)));\n303:  *             }\n304:  *         }\n305:  *     } finally {\n306:  *       consumer.close();\n307:  *     }\n308:  * </pre>\n309:  *\n310:  * <b>Note: The committed offset should always be the offset of the next message that your application will read.</b>\n311:  * Thus, when calling {@link #commitSync(Map) commitSync(offsets)} you should add one to the offset of the last message processed.\n312:  *\n313:  * <h4><a name=\"manualassignment\">Manual Partition Assignment</a></h4>\n314:  *\n315:  * In the previous examples, we subscribed to the topics we were interested in and let Kafka dynamically assign a\n316:  * fair share of the partitions for those topics based on the active consumers in the group. However, in\n317:  * some cases you may need finer control over the specific partitions that are assigned. For example:\n318:  * <p>\n319:  * <ul>\n320:  * <li>If the process is maintaining some kind of local state associated with that partition (like a\n321:  * local on-disk key-value store), then it should only get records for the partition it is maintaining on disk.\n322:  * <li>If the process itself is highly available and will be restarted if it fails (perhaps using a\n323:  * cluster management framework like YARN, Mesos, or AWS facilities, or as part of a stream processing framework). In\n324:  * this case there is no need for Kafka to detect the failure and reassign the partition since the consuming process\n325:  * will be restarted on another machine.\n326:  * </ul>\n327:  * <p>\n328:  * To use this mode, instead of subscribing to the topic using {@link #subscribe(Collection) subscribe}, you just call\n329:  * {@link #assign(Collection)} with the full list of partitions that you want to consume.\n330:  *\n331:  * <pre>\n332:  *     String topic = &quot;foo&quot;;\n333:  *     TopicPartition partition0 = new TopicPartition(topic, 0);\n334:  *     TopicPartition partition1 = new TopicPartition(topic, 1);\n335:  *     consumer.assign(Arrays.asList(partition0, partition1));\n336:  * </pre>\n337:  *\n338:  * Once assigned, you can call {@link #poll(Duration) poll} in a loop, just as in the preceding examples to consume\n339:  * records. The group that the consumer specifies is still used for committing offsets, but now the set of partitions\n340:  * will only change with another call to {@link #assign(Collection) assign}. Manual partition assignment does\n341:  * not use group coordination, so consumer failures will not cause assigned partitions to be rebalanced. Each consumer\n342:  * acts independently even if it shares a groupId with another consumer. To avoid offset commit conflicts, you should\n343:  * usually ensure that the groupId is unique for each consumer instance.\n344:  * <p>\n345:  * Note that it isn't possible to mix manual partition assignment (i.e. using {@link #assign(Collection) assign})\n346:  * with dynamic partition assignment through topic subscription (i.e. using {@link #subscribe(Collection) subscribe}).\n347:  *\n348:  * <h4><a name=\"rebalancecallback\">Storing Offsets Outside Kafka</h4>\n349:  *\n350:  * The consumer application need not use Kafka's built-in offset storage, it can store offsets in a store of its own\n351:  * choosing. The primary use case for this is allowing the application to store both the offset and the results of the\n352:  * consumption in the same system in a way that both the results and offsets are stored atomically. This is not always\n353:  * possible, but when it is it will make the consumption fully atomic and give \"exactly once\" semantics that are\n354:  * stronger than the default \"at-least once\" semantics you get with Kafka's offset commit functionality.\n355:  * <p>\n356:  * Here are a couple of examples of this type of usage:\n357:  * <ul>\n358:  * <li>If the results of the consumption are being stored in a relational database, storing the offset in the database\n359:  * as well can allow committing both the results and offset in a single transaction. Thus either the transaction will\n360:  * succeed and the offset will be updated based on what was consumed or the result will not be stored and the offset\n361:  * won't be updated.\n362:  * <li>If the results are being stored in a local store it may be possible to store the offset there as well. For\n363:  * example a search index could be built by subscribing to a particular partition and storing both the offset and the\n364:  * indexed data together. If this is done in a way that is atomic, it is often possible to have it be the case that even\n365:  * if a crash occurs that causes unsync'd data to be lost, whatever is left has the corresponding offset stored as well.\n366:  * This means that in this case the indexing process that comes back having lost recent updates just resumes indexing\n367:  * from what it has ensuring that no updates are lost.\n368:  * </ul>\n369:  * <p>\n370:  * Each record comes with its own offset, so to manage your own offset you just need to do the following:\n371:  *\n372:  * <ul>\n373:  * <li>Configure <code>enable.auto.commit=false</code>\n374:  * <li>Use the offset provided with each {@link ConsumerRecord} to save your position.\n375:  * <li>On restart restore the position of the consumer using {@link #seek(TopicPartition, long)}.\n376:  * </ul>\n377:  *\n378:  * <p>\n379:  * This type of usage is simplest when the partition assignment is also done manually (this would be likely in the\n380:  * search index use case described above). If the partition assignment is done automatically special care is\n381:  * needed to handle the case where partition assignments change. This can be done by providing a\n382:  * {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener} instance in the call to {@link #subscribe(Collection, ConsumerRebalanceListener)}\n383:  * and {@link #subscribe(Pattern, ConsumerRebalanceListener)}.\n384:  * For example, when partitions are taken from a consumer the consumer will want to commit its offset for those partitions by\n385:  * implementing {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener#onPartitionsRevoked(Collection)}. When partitions are assigned to a\n386:  * consumer, the consumer will want to look up the offset for those new partitions and correctly initialize the consumer\n387:  * to that position by implementing {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener#onPartitionsAssigned(Collection)}.\n388:  * <p>\n389:  * Another common use for {@link ConsumerRebalanceListener} is to flush any caches the application maintains for\n390:  * partitions that are moved elsewhere.\n391:  *\n392:  * <h4>Controlling The Consumer's Position</h4>\n393:  *\n394:  * In most use cases the consumer will simply consume records from beginning to end, periodically committing its\n395:  * position (either automatically or manually). However Kafka allows the consumer to manually control its position,\n396:  * moving forward or backwards in a partition at will. This means a consumer can re-consume older records, or skip to\n397:  * the most recent records without actually consuming the intermediate records.\n398:  * <p>\n399:  * There are several instances where manually controlling the consumer's position can be useful.\n400:  * <p>\n401:  * One case is for time-sensitive record processing it may make sense for a consumer that falls far enough behind to not\n402:  * attempt to catch up processing all records, but rather just skip to the most recent records.\n403:  * <p>\n404:  * Another use case is for a system that maintains local state as described in the previous section. In such a system\n405:  * the consumer will want to initialize its position on start-up to whatever is contained in the local store. Likewise\n406:  * if the local state is destroyed (say because the disk is lost) the state may be recreated on a new machine by\n407:  * re-consuming all the data and recreating the state (assuming that Kafka is retaining sufficient history).\n408:  * <p>\n409:  * Kafka allows specifying the position using {@link #seek(TopicPartition, long)} to specify the new position. Special\n410:  * methods for seeking to the earliest and latest offset the server maintains are also available (\n411:  * {@link #seekToBeginning(Collection)} and {@link #seekToEnd(Collection)} respectively).\n412:  *\n413:  * <h4>Consumption Flow Control</h4>\n414:  *\n415:  * If a consumer is assigned multiple partitions to fetch data from, it will try to consume from all of them at the same time,\n416:  * effectively giving these partitions the same priority for consumption. However in some cases consumers may want to\n417:  * first focus on fetching from some subset of the assigned partitions at full speed, and only start fetching other partitions\n418:  * when these partitions have few or no data to consume.\n419:  *\n420:  * <p>\n421:  * One of such cases is stream processing, where processor fetches from two topics and performs the join on these two streams.\n422:  * When one of the topics is long lagging behind the other, the processor would like to pause fetching from the ahead topic\n423:  * in order to get the lagging stream to catch up. Another example is bootstraping upon consumer starting up where there are\n424:  * a lot of history data to catch up, the applications usually want to get the latest data on some of the topics before consider\n425:  * fetching other topics.\n426:  *\n427:  * <p>\n428:  * Kafka supports dynamic controlling of consumption flows by using {@link #pause(Collection)} and {@link #resume(Collection)}\n429:  * to pause the consumption on the specified assigned partitions and resume the consumption\n430:  * on the specified paused partitions respectively in the future {@link #poll(Duration)} calls.\n431:  *\n432:  * <h3>Reading Transactional Messages</h3>\n433:  *\n434:  * <p>\n435:  * Transactions were introduced in Kafka 0.11.0 wherein applications can write to multiple topics and partitions atomically.\n436:  * In order for this to work, consumers reading from these partitions should be configured to only read committed data.\n437:  * This can be achieved by setting the {@code isolation.level=read_committed} in the consumer's configuration.\n438:  *\n439:  * <p>\n440:  * In <code>read_committed</code> mode, the consumer will read only those transactional messages which have been\n441:  * successfully committed. It will continue to read non-transactional messages as before. There is no client-side\n442:  * buffering in <code>read_committed</code> mode. Instead, the end offset of a partition for a <code>read_committed</code>\n443:  * consumer would be the offset of the first message in the partition belonging to an open transaction. This offset\n444:  * is known as the 'Last Stable Offset'(LSO).</p>\n445:  *\n446:  * <p>\n447:  * A {@code read_committed} consumer will only read up to the LSO and filter out any transactional\n448:  * messages which have been aborted. The LSO also affects the behavior of {@link #seekToEnd(Collection)} and\n449:  * {@link #endOffsets(Collection)} for {@code read_committed} consumers, details of which are in each method's documentation.\n450:  * Finally, the fetch lag metrics are also adjusted to be relative to the LSO for {@code read_committed} consumers.\n451:  *\n452:  * <p>\n453:  * Partitions with transactional messages will include commit or abort markers which indicate the result of a transaction.\n454:  * There markers are not returned to applications, yet have an offset in the log. As a result, applications reading from\n455:  * topics with transactional messages will see gaps in the consumed offsets. These missing messages would be the transaction\n456:  * markers, and they are filtered out for consumers in both isolation levels. Additionally, applications using\n457:  * {@code read_committed} consumers may also see gaps due to aborted transactions, since those messages would not\n458:  * be returned by the consumer and yet would have valid offsets.\n459:  *\n460:  * <h3><a name=\"multithreaded\">Multi-threaded Processing</a></h3>\n461:  *\n462:  * The Kafka consumer is NOT thread-safe. All network I/O happens in the thread of the application\n463:  * making the call. It is the responsibility of the user to ensure that multi-threaded access\n464:  * is properly synchronized. Un-synchronized access will result in {@link ConcurrentModificationException}.\n465:  *\n466:  * <p>\n467:  * The only exception to this rule is {@link #wakeup()}, which can safely be used from an external thread to\n468:  * interrupt an active operation. In this case, a {@link org.apache.kafka.common.errors.WakeupException} will be\n469:  * thrown from the thread blocking on the operation. This can be used to shutdown the consumer from another thread.\n470:  * The following snippet shows the typical pattern:\n471:  *\n472:  * <pre>\n473:  * public class KafkaConsumerRunner implements Runnable {\n474:  *     private final AtomicBoolean closed = new AtomicBoolean(false);\n475:  *     private final KafkaConsumer consumer;\n476:  *\n477:  *     public KafkaConsumerRunner(KafkaConsumer consumer) {\n478:  *       this.consumer = consumer;\n479:  *     }\n480:  *\n481:  *     {@literal}@Override\n482:  *     public void run() {\n483:  *         try {\n484:  *             consumer.subscribe(Arrays.asList(\"topic\"));\n485:  *             while (!closed.get()) {\n486:  *                 ConsumerRecords records = consumer.poll(Duration.ofMillis(10000));\n487:  *                 // Handle new records\n488:  *             }\n489:  *         } catch (WakeupException e) {\n490:  *             // Ignore exception if closing\n491:  *             if (!closed.get()) throw e;\n492:  *         } finally {\n493:  *             consumer.close();\n494:  *         }\n495:  *     }\n496:  *\n497:  *     // Shutdown hook which can be called from a separate thread\n498:  *     public void shutdown() {\n499:  *         closed.set(true);\n500:  *         consumer.wakeup();\n501:  *     }\n502:  * }\n503:  * </pre>\n504:  *\n505:  * Then in a separate thread, the consumer can be shutdown by setting the closed flag and waking up the consumer.\n506:  *\n507:  * <p>\n508:  * <pre>\n509:  *     closed.set(true);\n510:  *     consumer.wakeup();\n511:  * </pre>\n512:  *\n513:  * <p>\n514:  * Note that while it is possible to use thread interrupts instead of {@link #wakeup()} to abort a blocking operation\n515:  * (in which case, {@link InterruptException} will be raised), we discourage their use since they may cause a clean\n516:  * shutdown of the consumer to be aborted. Interrupts are mainly supported for those cases where using {@link #wakeup()}\n517:  * is impossible, e.g. when a consumer thread is managed by code that is unaware of the Kafka client.\n518:  *\n519:  * <p>\n520:  * We have intentionally avoided implementing a particular threading model for processing. This leaves several\n521:  * options for implementing multi-threaded processing of records.\n522:  *\n523:  * <h4>1. One Consumer Per Thread</h4>\n524:  *\n525:  * A simple option is to give each thread its own consumer instance. Here are the pros and cons of this approach:\n526:  * <ul>\n527:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It is the easiest to implement\n528:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It is often the fastest as no inter-thread co-ordination is needed\n529:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: It makes in-order processing on a per-partition basis very easy to implement (each thread just\n530:  * processes messages in the order it receives them).\n531:  * <li><b>CON</b>: More consumers means more TCP connections to the cluster (one per thread). In general Kafka handles\n532:  * connections very efficiently so this is generally a small cost.\n533:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Multiple consumers means more requests being sent to the server and slightly less batching of data\n534:  * which can cause some drop in I/O throughput.\n535:  * <li><b>CON</b>: The number of total threads across all processes will be limited by the total number of partitions.\n536:  * </ul>\n537:  *\n538:  * <h4>2. Decouple Consumption and Processing</h4>\n539:  *\n540:  * Another alternative is to have one or more consumer threads that do all data consumption and hands off\n541:  * {@link ConsumerRecords} instances to a blocking queue consumed by a pool of processor threads that actually handle\n542:  * the record processing.\n543:  *\n544:  * This option likewise has pros and cons:\n545:  * <ul>\n546:  * <li><b>PRO</b>: This option allows independently scaling the number of consumers and processors. This makes it\n547:  * possible to have a single consumer that feeds many processor threads, avoiding any limitation on partitions.\n548:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Guaranteeing order across the processors requires particular care as the threads will execute\n549:  * independently an earlier chunk of data may actually be processed after a later chunk of data just due to the luck of\n550:  * thread execution timing. For processing that has no ordering requirements this is not a problem.\n551:  * <li><b>CON</b>: Manually committing the position becomes harder as it requires that all threads co-ordinate to ensure\n552:  * that processing is complete for that partition.\n553:  * </ul>\n554:  *\n555:  * There are many possible variations on this approach. For example each processor thread can have its own queue, and\n556:  * the consumer threads can hash into these queues using the TopicPartition to ensure in-order consumption and simplify\n557:  * commit.\n558:  */\n559: public class KafkaConsumer<K, V> implements Consumer<K, V> {\n560: \n561:     private static final String CLIENT_ID_METRIC_TAG = \"client-id\";\n562:     private static final See the exception for more details\n1478:      * @throws java.lang.IllegalArgumentException if the committed offset is negative\n1479:      * @throws org.apache.kafka.common.KafkaException for any other unrecoverable errors (e.g. if offset metadata\n1480:      *             is too large or if the topic does not exist).\n1481:      * @throws org.apache.kafka.common.errors.TimeoutException if the timeout expires before successful completion\n1482:      *            of the offset commit\n1483:      * @throws org.apache.kafka.common.errors.FencedInstanceIdException if this consumer instance gets fenced by broker.\n1484:      */\n1485:     @Override\n1486:     public void commitSync(final Map<TopicPartition, OffsetAndMetadata> offsets, final Duration timeout) {\n1487:         acquireAndEnsureOpen();\n1488:         long commitStart = time.nanoseconds();\n1489:         try {\n1490:             maybeThrowInvalidGroupIdException();\n1491:             offsets.forEach(this::updateLastSeenEpochIfNewer);\n1492:             if (!coordinator.commitOffsetsSync(new HashMap<>(offsets), time.timer(timeout))) {\n1493:                 throw new TimeoutException(\"Timeout of \" + timeout.toMillis() + \"ms expired before successfully \" +\n1494:                         \"committing offsets \" + offsets);\n1495:             }\n1496:         } finally {\n1497:             kafkaConsumerMetrics.recordCommitSync(time.nanoseconds() - commitStart);\n1498:             release();\n1499:         }\n1500:     }\n1501: \n1502:     /**\n1503:      * Commit offsets returned on the last {@link #poll(Duration)} for all the subscribed list of topics and partition.\n1504:      * Same as {@link #commitAsync(OffsetCommitCallback) commitAsync(null)}\n1505:      * @throws org.apache.kafka.common.errors.FencedInstanceIdException if this consumer instance gets fenced by broker.\n1506:      */\n1507:     @Override\n1508:     public void commitAsync() {\n1509:         commitAsync(null);\n1510:     }\n1511: \n1512:     /**\n1513:      * Commit offsets returned on the last {@link #poll(Duration) poll()} for the subscribed list of topics and partitions.\n1514:      * <p>\n1515:      * This commits offsets only to Kafka. The offsets committed using this API will be used on the first fetch after\n1516:      * every rebalance and also on startup. As such, if you need to store offsets in anything other than Kafka, this API\n1517:      * should not be used.\n1518:      * <p>\n1519:      * This is an asynchronous call and will not block. Any errors encountered are either passed to the callback\n1520:      * (if provided) or discarded.\n1521:      * <p>\n1522:      * Offsets committed through multiple calls to this API are guaranteed to be sent in the same order as\n1523:      * the invocations. Corresponding commit callbacks are also invoked in the same order. Additionally note that\n1524:      * offsets committed through this API are guaranteed to complete before a subsequent call to {@link #commitSync()}\n1525:      * (and variants) returns.\n1526:      *\n1527:      * @param callback Callback to invoke when the commit completes\n1528:      * @throws org.apache.kafka.common.errors.FencedInstanceIdException if this consumer instance gets fenced by broker.\n1529:      */\n1530:     @Override\n1531:     public void commitAsync(OffsetCommitCallback callback) {\n1532:         commitAsync(subscriptions.allConsumed(), callback);\n1533:     }\n1534: \n1535:     /**\n1536:      * Commit the specified offsets for the specified list of topics and partitions to Kafka.\n1537:      * <p>\n1538:      * This commits offsets to Kafka. The offsets committed using this API will be used on the first fetch after every\n1539:      * rebalance and also on startup. As such, if you need to store offsets in anything other than Kafka, this API\n1540:      * should not be used. The committed offset should be the next message your application will consume,\n1541:      * i.e. lastProcessedMessageOffset + 1. If automatic group management with {@link #subscribe(Collection)} is used,\n1542:      * then the committed offsets must belong to the currently auto-assigned partitions.\n1543:      * <p>\n1544:      * This is an asynchronous call and will not block. Any errors encountered are either passed to the callback\n1545:      * (if provided) or discarded.\n1546:      * <p>\n1547:      * Offsets committed through multiple calls to this API are guaranteed to be sent in the same order as\n1548:      * the invocations. Corresponding commit callbacks are also invoked in the same order. Additionally note that\n1549:      * offsets committed through this API are guaranteed to complete before a subsequent call to {@link #commitSync()}\n1550:      * (and variants) returns.\n1551:      *\n1552:      * @param offsets A map of offsets by partition with associate metadata. This map will be copied internally, so it\n1553:      *                is safe to mutate the map after returning.\n1554:      * @param callback Callback to invoke when the commit completes\n1555:      * @throws org.apache.kafka.common.errors.FencedInstanceIdException if this consumer instance gets fenced by broker.\n1556:      */\n1557:     @Override\n1558:     public void commitAsync(final Map<TopicPartition, OffsetAndMetadata> offsets, OffsetCommitCallback callback) {\n1559:         acquireAndEnsureOpen();\n1560:         try {\n1561:             maybeThrowInvalidGroupIdException();\n1562:             log.debug(\"Committing offsets: {}\", offsets);\n1563:             offsets.forEach(this::updateLastSeenEpochIfNewer);\n1564:             coordinator.commitOffsetsAsync(new HashMap<>(offsets), callback);\n1565:         } finally {\n1566:             release();\n1567:         }\n1568:     }\n1569: \n1570:     /**\n1571:      * Overrides the fetch offsets that the consumer will use on the next {@link #poll(Duration) poll(timeout)}. If this API\n1572:      * is invoked for the same partition more than once, the latest offset will be used on the next poll(). Note that\n1573:      * you may lose data if this API is arbitrarily used in the middle of consumption, to reset the fetch offsets\n1574:      *\n1575:      * @throws IllegalArgumentException if the provided offset is negative\n1576:      * @throws IllegalStateException if the provided TopicPartition is not assigned to this consumer\n1577:      */\n1578:     @Override\n1579:     public void seek(TopicPartition partition, long offset) {\n1580:         if (offset < 0)\n1581:             throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"seek offset must not be a negative number\");\n1582: \n1583:         acquireAndEnsureOpen();\n1584:         try {\n1585:             log.info(\"Seeking to offset {} for partition {}\", offset, partition);\n1586:             SubscriptionState.FetchPosition newPosition = new SubscriptionState.FetchPosition(\n1587:                     offset,\n1588:                     Optional.empty(), // This will ensure we skip validation\n1589:                     this.metadata.currentLeader(partition));\n1590:             this.subscriptions.seekUnvalidated(partition, newPosition);\n1591:         } finally {\n1592:             release();\n1593:         }\n1594:     }\n1595: \n1596:     /**\n1597:      * Overrides the fetch offsets that the consumer will use on the next {@link #poll(Duration) poll(timeout)}. If this API\n1598:      * is invoked for the same partition more than once, the latest offset will be used on the next poll(). Note that\n1599:      * you may lose data if this API is arbitrarily used in the middle of consumption, to reset the fetch offsets. This\n1600:      * method allows for setting the leaderEpoch along with the desired offset.\n1601: ","url":"https://sourcegraph.sourcegraph.com/github.com/sourcegraph-testing/kafka@a61b998c5df7ca9baccc5d966cb1e95108cbdee9/-/blob/clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/clients/consumer/KafkaConsumer.java%3FL1300-1600"}}}
